Acter (5536 TT): 7 October 2026 
global supply chains restructure under N+1 (domestic market + 1) strategies, companies 
building overseas facilities in places like the US face strict and complicated licensing 
processes, particularly on fire safety and hazardous chemicals. Without experience dealing 
with such engineering and safety issues, construction timelines can be severely delayed.

As a result, even with advanced equipment and chip designs, a high-tech company cannot 
begin production without adequate cleanroom space, skilled engineers, and system-
integration capabilities. Therefore, cleanroom engineering contractors hold significant 
bargaining power and capacity allocation leverage in today's tech supply chain.

IC foundry 
In the foundry sector, where the cleanroom supply-demand shortage is the tightest, project 
activity remains centred on advanced process capacity expansion in Taiwan and Arizona. 
Simultaneously, domestic advanced node development remains intense, with multi-phase 
fab builds advancing across Hsinchu, Taichung, and Kaohsiung. Local cleanroom and MEP 
integration demand is further boosted by advanced packaging facilities in Taiwan (such as 
Chiayi and Tainan) and upcoming projects in the US are set to contribute revenue for the 
cleanroom providers in the following years.

Memory 
The memory segment is exhibiting robust momentum as well, mainly catalysed by HBM 
capacity additions. Projects such as the plant in Japan, transitioned to contribution for the 
cleanroom EPCM providers starting from 2026. In Taiwan, turnkey engineering contracts in 
Miaoli are also actively underway. This trend is reinforced by stronger-than-expected 
expansion activity from memory producers in China.

OSAT,  PCB and ODM/OEM 
For OSAT companies, demand is also surging due to the spill-over effect of advanced 
packaging protocols and N+1 supply chain restructuring. The OSAT names are expanding 
their footprint both in Southern Taiwan and in overseas locations including in the US and 
Southeast Asia. Major engineering contracts signed between OSAT clients and Acter show 
stable long-term workload for strong cleanroom demand over our forecast horizon. 
Similarly, overseas expansion for PCB manufacturing is peaking in Southeast Asia, 
especially in Thailand. We expect Chinese PCB players to also increase their capacity, just 
like memory companies in the past year. Except for front-end and back-end chip 
manufacturing, ODM/OEM and data centre infrastructure is also a key growth engine for 
the EPCM industry. Major Taiwanese ODMs are building production facilities in Texas and 
Mexico to meet local manufacturing needs. Cleanroom contractors, such as Acter, 
providing MEP and system integration in Texas are directly benefiting from these projects. 
The shift toward liquid cooling systems has also increased engineering complexity, 
favouring few cleanroom companies with the expertise to deliver complex integration for 
CSPs in both North America and Southeast Asia.

Acter: an integrated cleanroom solutions provider  
Acter Group (Acter), an integrated cleanroom solutions provider, maintains its competitive 
positioning within the high-tech cleanroom and MEP engineering sector, driven by its group 
synergy and global footprint. Acter operates as a comprehensive vendor through a group 
business model. The parent company, Acter, supervises overall cleanroom and MEP 
services, while subsidiary Nova Technology (Novatech, 6613 TT, not rated) mainly provides 
chemical primary system and hook-up, and Novatech’s subsidiary Rayzher (7703 TT, not 
rated) provides gas primary system, hook-up and equipment. In China, Acter operates 
through Acter Technology Integration Group (603163 CH, not rated) and Suzhou Winmax 
(not listed) for Chinese EPCM (engineering, procurement, construction, and management) 
and equipment business. Except for C&S (civil and structural) works, where the margin is 
low (7-8%) and the contract duration is long, Acter’s services span nearly the entire 
cleanroom construction process. Its one-stop solutions model supports customer retention 
Acter provides

solutions via various 
subsidiaries

Acter (5536 TT): 7 October 2026 
and improves bid-win rates, particularly for overseas expansion, where customers have 
few reliable providers to choose from.

Acter: organisational chart

Source: Company, Daiwa

To avoid the risk of over-concentration, Acter diversifies its client portfolio across 
semiconductor foundries, memory, PCB, OSAT, ODM hardware assembly, CSP data 
centres and the consumer industry. By sector, semiconductor projects represent the largest 
share of Acter’s portfolio, accounting for 66% of total revenue in 1H26, vs. 69% in 2025. 
Foundry manufacturing is the largest source of demand for cleanrooms in Taiwan, 
especially as advanced-node capacity expands at sites in Hsinchu, Taichung and 
Kaohsiung. In the memory segment, the company executes turnkey engineering builds to 
expand its capacity aggressively, creating cleanroom opportunities across Taiwan, Japan 
and Singapore. This multi-sector coverage decreases the risk of cyclical downturns in any 
individual segment, in our view.

By establishing 33 operating entities across 11 international regions, the company 
effectively captures capex migration driven by global N+1 supply chain diversification. This 
allows Acter to support Taiwanese and international clients as they expand manufacturing 
capacity from Taiwan into Southeast Asia, Japan and the US. Due to the complexity of 
advanced-node IC manufacturing, packaging and testing, it is especially important for 
these Taiwanese semiconductor companies to work with familiar cleanroom builders when 
expanding in the US. This is one of the reasons why Taiwanese cleanroom companies are 
receiving overseas orders and starting to set up global bases, in our view. Despite higher 
management, material, and manpower costs in the US, most cleanroom companies are 
benefitting from the trend by improving margins, given the higher complexity and risks to 
operate overseas. Across overseas hubs in Texas and Phoenix (expected to begin from 
2027), Acter prioritises local hiring and partners with regional civil contractors to shorten 
the learning curve and to avoid the high overhead. To further address the potential labour 
shortages in the future, the company has established cross-border academic and 
vocational partnerships to bring Southeast Asian talent to Taiwan for technical training 
before deploying them back to their home markets, securing future human capacity to 
support global expansion projects.

By product, cleanroom electromechanical services remain Acter’s largest source of 
revenue (accounting for 66% of 1H26 revenue, per the company). These services include 
cleanroom architectural and MEP engineering, which powers airflow, filtration, 
environmental regulation and utility controls. Water, gas and chemical delivery systems are 
its second-largest source of revenue (accounting for 24% of 1H26 revenue, per the 
company). Contributed mainly by Novatech (6613 TT, not rated) and Rayzher (7703 TT, not 
rated), these services cover the main systems and hook-up systems, for  ultra-pure water 
(UPW), specialty gases, chemical piping, etc., all of which require highly precise build-up 
work. Except for cleanroom electromechanical and water, gas and chemical delivery 
systems, the rest of Acter’s revenue comes from the consumer industry services 
electromechanical (5%), biomedical (3%), and high-tech equipment and materials sales 
and services (1%).

Acter: revenue by product

Source: Company, Daiwa

Technically, Acter integrates digital transformation and green engineering into its business 
model, helping it differentiate from peers. Acter deploys digital twin technology and 1:1 3D 
BIM modelling to detect piping interferences and optimise spatial layout before installation, 
minimising schedule delays. This makes Acter a more reliable option, in our view, and thus 
being a favourable supplier, especially for the semiconductor clients that have extremely 
high quality and swiftness criterion.

Bright outlook: strong order visibility 
Rising orders on hand due to supply-demand imbalance for cleanrooms 
Acter's order backlog has increased from TWD55bn in 1Q26 to TWD60bn in 2Q26 and 
TWD66bn in July 2026. We expect this figure to further increase in the following quarters, 
given the supply-demand imbalance in the cleanroom sector. This is composed of 
semiconductor (63%), electronic assembly (11%), consumer (9%) and others (17%), per 
the company. In 2H25, the company accelerated the digestion of small-scale projects 
below TWD1bn, and, as a result, starting from 1H26, it successfully shifted its project

Back-end packaging, cloud infrastructure and hardware assembly are additional growth 
drivers. Acter serves as a contractor for both Taiwanese and overseas OSAT providers, 
including TWD21.7bn contracts YTD, awarded by a major OSAT client vs. TWD16.6bn 
contract obtained from the same client in 2025. In cloud infrastructure, Acter maintains 
qualified supplier status with US CSPs, providing mechanical, electrical and plumbing 
(MEP) integration, especially for those data centres that implement liquid-cooling systems. 
The company also wins factory construction projects for Taiwanese ODMs across the US 
and Southeast Asia, as well as PCB facility projects in Thailand and China. Non-tech 
sectors, including biotechnology, commercial buildings, smart logistics hubs and medical 
facilities, comprise 9% of the total backlog, per management.

Competitive advantages 
High entry barriers to the segment 
Comprehensive product portfolio, empowered by subsidiaries 
Acter provides a one-stop EPCM solution, leveraging its subsidiaries to deliver complete 
solutions. Within the group, the parent company Acter focuses on overall cleanroom and 
MEP system integration; subsidiary Novatech provides primary chemical systems and 
hook-up systems; and sub-subsidiary Rayzer specialises in primary specialty-gas systems 
and hook-up systems. In China, Suzhou Winmax, also a subsidiary of Novatech, focuses 
on the supply of wet processing and chemical handling equipment. This integrated model 
covers cleanroom engineering integration, water, power, chemical and gas-utility systems, 
as well as post-completion validation and maintenance, ensuring cross-system 
compatibility while mitigating risks associated with multi-contractor hiring.

Under global supply chain diversification trends, this unified group strategy strengthens 
order-winning capabilities in overseas markets. Clients expanding internationally face 
regulatory, linguistic and supply-chain complexities, favouring partners that offer one-stop 
services with a proven cross-border track record.

A diverse client base helps Acter weather economic cycles 
Acter’s main competitive advantage is its diversification of clients by industry and region. 
This guides the company through the peaks and troughs of the economy, which is 
especially crucial given the strong cyclicality of the semiconductor/high-tech industry.  Acter 
serves a broad client base across semiconductors, OSAT, PCB, electronics assembly 
(ODM), CSP data centres, biopharmaceuticals and even the consumer industry (including 
commercial real estate and logistics), whereas many peers rely on a few large foundry 
clients. When semiconductor demand slows, capacity expansion in the PCB or data centre 
sectors offset the decline. Additionally, Acter avoids concentration risk and margin 
compression from major foundry accounts, as projects from leading foundries yield lower 
margins; by serving niche markets like US-based CSPs, the company achieves higher 
gross margins.

Geographically, Acter operates across Taiwan, China, Southeast Asia, the US and other 
countries. The company has OSAT and foundry customers in Taiwan and China, PCB 
clients expanding into Thailand, and ODM and data centre clients establishing operations 
in the US. Across different countries, local hiring and local supply chain implementation 
can further reduce costs and increase efficiency relative to peers reliant on a cross-border 
labour force.

Labour shortages 
Amid global labour shortages, Acter minimises the operational impact and turns workforce 
constraints into a moat through localised talent development. Since engineering labour 
cannot easily be automated, Acter has built a cross-border talent pipeline via industry-
academia partnerships. The company recruits Southeast Asian students for specialised 
M&E engineering training in Taiwan, deploying them to global project sites after their 
graduation to solve labour bottlenecks. This structure maintains an engineering team with 
an average tenure of more than 7 years, creating an operational barrier in an industry with 
a high employee turnover rate.

Compared with peers relying on high-cost personnel sent from Taiwan, we note that Acter’s 
local hiring strategy creates cost and efficiency advantages. Prioritising compliant local 
hiring across overseas markets reduces travel expenses while accelerating integration with 
local supply chains and regulations. Localised teams also handle post-completion services 
and technical support directly on-site, building long-term client trust in overseas markets.

The supply of cleanroom 
services is limited due to

segment

### Page 11

Acter (5536 TT): 7 October 2026 
Numerous competitors, each with different specialisation 
Acter faces different competitors across its main product lines. In the EPCM segment, 
including cleanroom and MEP works, key competitors include UIS (2404 TT, not rated), 
L&K (6139 TT, not rated), Yankey (6691 TT, not rated) and Jiuhan (6903 TT, not rated). 
Despite having business models similar to Acter, UIS, Yankey and Jiuhan focus more on 
major semiconductor players in both Taiwan and the US, and L&K concentrates on ASEAN 
semiconductor projects. In the equipment/material sales and agency space, Acter mainly 
competes with Marketech (6196 TT, not rated). In the water, gas and chemical delivery 
systems area, through Novatech and Rayzher, Acter group competes with Marketech, 
Mega Union (6944 TT, not rated), Taiwan Puritic (6826 TT, not rated), Trusval (6667 TT, not 
rated) and Cica-Huntek (6725 TT, not rated).

In our view, despite the competitive environment, Acter maintains a formidable competitive 
moat built on several key advantages. We attribute this to its more than 45 year experience 
in the industry, enabling it to develop specialised expertise that is hard for competitors to 
replicate.

Financial analysis 
We forecast the company’s net profit to rise at a CAGR of 36.9% over 2026-28E, driven by 
a revenue CAGR of 24.6% due to surging demand from the high-tech industry (an increase 
in the order backlog to TWD66bn at the end of July 2026 vs. TWD46bn at end-2025) and a 
3.3pp expansion in operating margin over 2026-28E, driven by the gross margin 
improvement from more favourable project wins and operating leverage.

Strong order visibility, with revenue expected to grow 
across all sectors over 2026-28E 
We forecast Acter’s revenue to increase at a CAGR of 24.6% over 2026-28E, driven by 
scale expansion and a growing demand from various industries, including semiconductor, 
electronic assembly, optoelectronics and others.

Acter: revenue growth trend

Source: Company, Daiwa forecasts

Gross- and operating-margin expansion trend  
Over 2026-28E, we forecast Acter’s gross and operating margins to expand on rising 
revenue scale and a higher complexity of cleanrooms for advanced manufacturing. We 
note that the gross margin from high-tech cleanrooms is higher than that from consumer 
industries, such as commercial buildings and biopharmaceuticals. Thus, we estimate the 
company’s gross margin to reach 26.0% in 2028E from 18.9% in 2025 and its operating 
margin to reach 21.6% in 2028E from 13.7% in 2025.

Acter: gross- and operating-margin trend

14.2% in 2028E (vs. 11.7% in 2026E) and for its net profit to grow at a CAGR of 36.9%

We forecast Acter’s total cash and equivalents to reach TWD16,838m (36.8% of its total

We believe the capacity expansion cost will initially be low and that the company will 
continue to benefit from the better profitability.

(following steady annual increases to 46.8% in 2026E and 54.9% in 2027E), which we 
attribute to margin expansion from the revenue size increase and gross margin expansion 
led by increases in cleanroom complexity.

Acter (5536 TT): 7 October 2026 
Valuation and risks  
Undemanding valuation, alongside its leading global 
positions in the OSAT and memory segments and an 
optimistic industry outlook  
We initiate coverage of Acter with a Buy (1) rating and 12-month TP of TWD1,415, based 
on a target PER of 20x applied to our one-year-forward EPS forecast. This target multiple 
is higher than the average of its past-3-year 1-year-forward PER of 12.2x (ranged from 
7.4x to 23.5x) and the average of its past-5-year 1-year-forward PER of 9.6x (ranged from 
4.4x to 23.5x), which we view as undemanding as we forecast a 36.9% net profit CAGR 
over 2026-28E, implying a 0.54x PEG. This is supported by its business expansion in the 
next 2-3 years. We see potential for a rerating of the stock, given Acter’s global leadership 
in cleanroom EPCM, particularly in the OSAT and memory industries.

The stock is trading currently at a 15.8x 1-year-forward PER vs. its earnings CAGR of 
36.9% over 2026-28E, translating to a 0.43x PEG ratio. Its share price has underperformed 
TAIEX Index by 32.7% in 3Q26, mainly due to its weaker-than-expected 2Q26 results. 
However, given the potential improvement in sector sentiment following TSMC’s results on 
15 October 2026, along with the likelihood that Acter’s revenue momentum will strengthen 
in the coming months, we recommend that investors buy the stock.

Acter: past-3-year-forward PER (average: 12.0x)    Acter: past-5-year-forward PER (average: 9.5x)

Source: Bloomberg, Daiwa estimates  Source: Bloomberg, Daiwa estimates

We compare Acter with other leading cleanroom EPCM companies such as UIS (2404 TT, 
TWD1,330.0, not rated), L&K (6139 TT, TWD787.0, not rated) and Yankey (6691 TT, 
TWD776.0, not rated). UIS, L&K and Yankey are trading at a PER of 14.7x (earnings 
CAGR of 40.7% over 2025-27E), 9.5x (earnings CAGR of 36.9% over 2026-28E) and 
15.1x (earnings CAGR of 53.6% over 2025-27E), on their 1-year-forward EPS, 
respectively.

Acter’s peers are trading at 18.7x for 2026E and 14.2x for 2027E. We forecast Acter to 
deliver an EPS CAGR of 36.9% over 2026-28E vs. 31.5% CAGR for the segment over the 
same period.

We believe our target one-year-forward PER of 20x (equivalent to a 0.54x PEG using 
2026-28E EPS CAGR) is undemanding as: 1) Acter is ramping up capacity to meet 
growing demand from high-tech customers and is in an expansion cycle; and 2) its 
leading position among OSAT and memory clients warrants a valuation premium, 
compared to its global peers.

Risks to our call  
Primary/key risks: 
Weaker-than-expected expansion in semiconductor industry 
The biggest downside risk to our call for Acter is weaker-than-expected expansion from 
semiconductor clients, especially in advanced-node foundries, advanced packaging and 
OSAT. If global demand for AI servers, HPC or HBM slows, or if clients delay fab build-ups 
and overseas expansion plans, it will directly pressure order inflows and backlog 
conversion for Acter.

Project execution delays or cancellations 
Acter’s revenue recognition is tied to large, complex EPCM and integrated engineering 
projects, which are often subject to customer-driven delays. Any postponement in project 
Our 12-month TP of 
TWD1,415 is also 
supported by our DCF 
valuation

### Page 18

18

Acter (5536 TT): 7 October 2026 
schedule, construction bottlenecks or cancellation of large projects could defer revenue 
recognition and hurt overall profit. This risk is more obvious in overseas projects, where 
licensing, fire-safety approvals and hazardous-chemical regulations are more complicated.

Higher-than-expected raw-material price hikes 
Raw-material costs (especially copper and oil-related costs) account for 50-60% of COGS 
and thus the price volatility makes costs difficult to manage. Acter needs to maintain 
supplier relations, negotiate appropriate price-adjustment mechanisms with clients, and 
invest in R&D to identify composite material substitutes, thereby reducing reliance on any 
single component.

Secondary risks:  
Labour issues or larger-than-expected increases in labour costs  
Cleanroom engineering is highly labour-intensive and relies on on-site installation and 
supervision. Although Acter has built a local hiring and talent-development model, a larger-
than-expected shortage of engineers or rising wage pressure could still raise costs and 
constrain capacity expansion. Any inability to secure enough skilled labour may also limit 
the company’s ability to convert orders into revenue in time.

Margin pressure from lower-margin projects or mix shift 
Acter’s business mix includes a few low-margin C&S services as well as larger EPCM and 
system integration contracts. If the company takes on a higher proportion of lower-margin 
projects, or if competition intensifies in certain geographies or customer segments, gross 
margin could come under pressure. In addition, an unfavourable product mix shift towards 
less profitable work could offset benefits from backlog growth.

Overseas expansion and regulatory risks 
Acter is expanding across markets in the US, Southeast Asia, Japan and China, which 
exposes it to FX movements, local compliance requirements, tax complexity and regulatory 
uncertainty. Delays in licensing, changes in safety standards or local-content requirements 
could increase project costs and reduce execution efficiency. Any misstep in managing 
overseas operations could damage profitability and client retention. 
Key risks to our call: 
weaker-than-expected 
expansion in 
semiconductor industry, 
project delays or 
cancellations, and 
higher-than-expected 
raw-material price hikes

Acter (5536 TT): 7 October 2026 
Appendix 
Company background 
History 
Acter (5536 TT), founded in 1979 and headquartered in Taichung, Taiwan, is a cleanroom 
EPCM provider with over four decades of experience. Starting with early mechanical and 
electrical (M&E) engineering through vertical and horizontal integration, Acter has now 
developed into an engineering technology group with cross-border execution capabilities. A 
cleanroom building process begins with civil & structural (C&S) works to establish the 
physical plant, followed by mechanical, electrical, and plumbing (MEP) works for 
essential utility generation, where Acter is one of the experts. After MEP is finished, 
chemicals/gas/water are then distributed via main systems through primary piping 
networks, and finally delivered to production equipment through hook-up systems. The 
company’s core competency is providing one-stop integrated total solutions, spanning 
services, primary piping networks and hook-up systems, leaving C&S services to other 
engineering peers, given the nature of the latter segment’s low margin and long timespan.

In terms of business model, Acter delivers EPCM (Engineering, Procurement, 
Construction, Management) capabilities. From initial customised planning and feasibility 
assessments to strategic procurement, installation, and post-completion maintenance, 
Acter builds production environments tailored to client process requirements.

Acter: EPCM solutions offered

Source: Company, Daiwa

Regarding global expansion, Acter actively responds to the global supply chain’s “N+1” 
strategy, operating across 11 regions with 33 companies globally. Its service network spans 
Taiwan, China, Southeast Asia (including Thailand, Malaysia, Vietnam, Indonesia, and 
Singapore), Japan, India, and the US. The company leverages a group synergy strategy 
by integrating resources from key subsidiaries – such as Novatech (6613 TT, not rated; 
provides chemical primary and hook-up systems), Rayzher (7703 TT, not rated; provides 
special gas primary and hook-up supply systems), and Acter Group (603163 CH, not rated; 
provides cleanroom engineering in China) – to form a complete technical ecosystem, 
ensuring cross-border capability and quality.

cleanroom EPCM 
provider in Taiwan; 
dominates both high-
tech market and

### Page 20

Acter (5536 TT): 7 October 2026 
Acter: global footprint

Source: Company, Daiwa

In 2Q26, Acter classified revenue as: cleanroom electromechanical (63%), water, gas and 
chemical delivery systems (28%), consumer industry electromechanical (6%), biomedical 
(2%) and high-tech equipment & materials sales & services (1%). In 2025, the product mix 
was: cleanroom electromechanical (71%), water, gas and chemical delivery systems 
(20%), consumer industry electromechanical (4%), biomedical (2%) and high-tech 
equipment & materials sales & services (3%).

Acter serves a diversified client base divided into high-tech and consumer divisions. The 
high-tech division focuses on advanced sectors including semiconductors, OSAT, PCB, AI 
data centres (for CSPs), and electronics assembly. The consumer division extends into 
healthcare, premium commercial properties, logistics facilities, and green engineering. This 
multi-industry strategy allows the company to capitalise on tech sector upgrades while 
maintaining resilient operations across technology (especially semiconductor) cycles.

Acter adopts a management strategy involving local hiring in overseas markets, prioritising 
collaboration with local supply chains and compliance with local regulations to reduce 
cross-border deployment costs and thus build strong local teams. Acter has more than 
2,600 employees worldwide (average age: 42 years; average length of service: 7 years). 
With more than 45 years of experience in EPCM, the company plays a reliable role for both 
high-tech and consumer industry.

recycling, and AI smart facility management systems to help clients achieve carbon 
neutrality while expanding capacity. Backed by solid corporate governance, Acter

Glossary of terms 
EPCM (engineering, procurement, construction, and management): Cleanroom 
construction services that cover the whole process including project design, purchasing, 
construction and management.

BIM (building information modelling): A digital modelling system of a building and its key 
information used in cleanroom construction.

C&S (civil & structural): Site infrastructure and the design and construction of load-
bearing elements.

MEP (mechanical, electrical, and plumbing): Building essential systems for environment 
control, power, lighting, water, and drainage.

### Page 22

Acter (5536 TT): 7 October 2026 
ESG analysis

Update Date: 7 Oct 2026 
Source:  Daiwa, Company

ESG risks
Risks Management Analyst comments
G
Executive/board
quality 1
Acter maintains a structured governance framework that supports disciplined management. The
chairman is Chin-Li Liang. The company operates with defined responsibility across the board,
management, cyber security oversight, operations, and audit functions. It has established an
organisation function led by the President, a Chief Cyber Security Officer, a Response Team, an
Operations and Policy Enforcement Team, and an Audit Team to supervise policy execution and
review security implementation.
Capital management 1
Acter manages capital in a stable and shareholder-friendly manner. Over the past several years,
Acter has typically maintained a dividend payout ratio of 65-80%. It also maintains a strong
financing network across multiple banks, supporting flexibility for project execution and
expansion while preserving financial stability.
Related party &
transaction 1
Acter’s business is supported by a transparent network of suppliers, project partners, and
financial counterparties, including companies with equity ties and reciprocal business
relationships that are common in its operating ecosystem. These relationships are managed
through formal internal procedures for fund lending, endorsements and guarantees, and asset
acquisition and disposal, all of which are approved through shareholder mechanisms and
company rules. Such a structure helps keep inter-company and supply-chain transactions
organised and traceable, reducing the risk of unclear dealings.
E Product quality &safety 1
Acter executes its projects through a complete, disciplined process covering bidding
assessment, project planning, design, procurement, construction management, verification and
testing, completion and acceptance, closing review, and warranty service. Its cleanroom and
electromechanical engineering business is built around maintaining temperature, humidity, and
dust-free environments that support high-precision manufacturing. The company strengthens
quality control through BIM, Revit, simulation software, ISO drawing output tools, and automated
modeling, which improve design accuracy, construction efficiency, and project consistency.
E Ecological impacts 1
Acter has built a concrete sustainability programme with measurable environmental outcomes.
The company completed Scope 1, 2, and 3 greenhouse gas emissions calculation, obtained
third-party verification under ISO 14064-1:2018, and publicly disclosed energy consumption and
emissions data. It also implemented a strong waste reduction programme, achieving a 95%
wastepaper recycling rate at headquarters in 2025 and 100% paper recycling at the 19F and 20F
floors after establishing paper segregation and reuse procedures. The company also promoted
sustainability measures through emissions inventory management, environmental disclosures,
and resource-efficiency practices. These actions show active environmental management and a
clear commitment to lowering operational impact.
Health & safety
Note: Management score represents a company's ability to manage/benefit from certain ESG topics. The scores range from 1 to 3, with 1 being the strongest.

Document 232: Entering the age of the older consumer As America ages, older consumers have more leisure time, significant wealth and growing economic influence. 2 min read

Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 8

### Page 1

Economy 
Entering the age of the older consumer 
01 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• The older consumer is moving center stage. America's population has been aging for decades, but the shift has accelerated: the 
share aged over 60 increased by almost 10 percentage points (pp) between 1995 and 2025. And the Census Bureau projects the 
share of the population over 60 will rise another 5pp by 2055, to stand at nearly 30% of the population. 
• More free time means a different spending mix. People aged 65 and over spend around two and a half fewer hours per day 
working and roughly two hours more on leisure than the overall adult population. Bank of America data shows that older 
households allocate relatively more of their card spending to groceries and travel, and less to restaurants, gasoline, general 
merchandise and clothing. 
• Households aged over 55 held close to $140 trillion in net worth in the second quarter of 2026, giving many older consumers 
considerable capacity to spend on travel and other leisure categories. But wealth is not evenly distributed, and healthcare and 
other cost pressures remain significant for older households on tighter or fixed incomes.

Population aging is nothing new, but it is accelerating  
The US population has been growing relatively “older” for a long time. Exhibit 1 shows that the proportion of the population 
under 20 years old shrank from the mid-1960s through 2025. At the same time, the proportion of the population in older age 
groups has been rising.  
Exhibit 1: Over time the US population has steadily aged  
Proportion of US population by age range (%)

Source: Human Mortality Database; Bank of America Institute calculations

Additionally, there has been a notable acceleration in the relative aging of the population in recent years. For example, between 
1995 and 2025, the proportion of the population aged over 60 rose by almost 10 percentage points (pp) to 25%.  
Several factors have driven this. First, fertility rates in the US, as in much of the world, have fallen sharply. This has slowed 
natural population growth and, by reducing the number of younger people relative to older cohorts, has increased the share of 
the population at older ages. Second, more recently, net inward migration to the US has slowed. Because migrants tend to be 
younger than the overall population, weaker immigration also tends to raise the average age. Finally, rising life expectancy over 
much of the post-war period has allowed more people to survive into older age, further increasing the older population's share.  
Exhibit 2: The trend towards an older composition of the population is expected to continue  
Forecast changes in the share of population by age group relative to 2025 (pp)

Source: United States Census Bureau 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Lower fertility and, more recently, weaker net migration look set to slow overall US population growth in the decades ahead. As a 
result, older Americans are likely to play an increasingly important role in the economy, both as a share of the population and, in 
many age groups, in absolute numbers. The Census Bureau projects the share of the population over 60 will rise another 5pp by 
2055, to stand at nearly 30% of the population (Exhibit 2). For businesses, understanding how the needs and spending patterns 
of older consumers differ from those of younger generations will therefore become increasingly relevant. 
Older age groups allocate their time differently  
Why does this matter? One reason is that older people tend to spend their time and money quite differently from younger adults. 
Most obviously, many work less or have retired altogether. Data from the American Time Use Survey (ATUS) shows that people 
aged 65 and over spend, on average, around two and a half fewer hours per day working than the population aged 15+ (Exhibit 
3). 
That leaves considerably more time for other activities. People aged 65 and over spend around two hours more per day on 
leisure and sports activities, with television viewing accounting for a particularly large share of this additional leisure time 
(Exhibit 4).

Older households also spend differently  
Bank of America internal data on credit and debit card spending provides insights into how these differences in older 
generations’ use of time also impact their spending. Rather than splitting our spending data by generation, as we often do, here 
we split by age bands, allowing for consistent comparisons over time.  
Exhibit 5 shows how the composition of card spending of households aged 61-75 and those older than 75 differs from that of all 
households. A few things stand out. For one, older households devote a greater share of their overall card spending to groceries 
(food and beverages). Households aged 61-75 also allocate more spending to travel (airlines and lodging). And restaurants and 
bars, gasoline, general merchandise and clothing account for a smaller share of older households’ card spending.  
These differences may seem fairly intuitive. Older households have more leisure hours, so spend relatively more on travel to 
enjoy them. Previously we found that more available time may support travel particularly around retirement, although the 
discretionary spending lift can be temporary (for more, read our publication: Paychecks to pensions: The evolution of retiree 
spending).  
Older households are also likely to have less need to update their work wardrobes, so they probably spend less on clothing.

Still, older households’ relatively lower spending on restaurants and bars is a little more curious, perhaps, given dining out could 
be one way of making use of more leisure time. Indeed, when we look at how these spending shares are evolving, we find a 
nuanced picture. While households aged 61-75 do spend relatively less on restaurants and bars than the overall population, they 
appear to be narrowing this gap. But for those older than 75 this is not the case. In our view, the pandemic may still be weighing 
on dining-out spending among this older age group (Exhibit 6).

Interestingly, however, the airline spending share of both older groups has recovered more strongly since the pandemic than that 
of all households, although it remains below its 2019 level (Exhibit 7).

Aging presents opportunities, but also challenges 
In our view, the aging of the US population presents important opportunities for the economy and businesses. Older consumers 
have more leisure time and businesses that cater to these demands may therefore benefit. 
Moreover, some older consumers hold considerable amounts of net wealth and have been some of the biggest beneficiaries of 
recent rises in equities. Using data from the Federal Reserve, Exhibit 8 shows that as of the second quarter of 2026 the net 
worth of those households aged 55 and over was close to $140 trillion, around three-quarters of the total. And with equity 
markets rising strongly, these age groups have seen a more than 20% increase in their net worth over the past two years, 
potentially enhancing their spending power further.  
But an important caveat is that not all older consumers are in this position. Previous Institute work found evidence consistent 
with equity-market wealth effects being concentrated toward higher-income households (read our publication: Paychecks to 
pensions: The evolution of retiree spending). And Census Bureau research has found that around 14% of Social Security 
recipients aged over 65 rely on it for over 90% of their income. For older households without a wealth cushion, recent elevated 
cost pressures, like gasoline, will likely have put them under more significant financial strain. They will also be more dependent 
on cost-of-living (COLA) increases in Social Security to underpin their spending.  
And, more broadly, outside of card spending, healthcare costs are a much larger factor in older households’ budgets (Exhibit 9). 
The evolution of these costs going forward will be a key variable for older consumers’ budgets.

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
The differences between the total and per household card spending growth rate (if discussed) can be explained by the following reasons:  
1. Overall total card spending growth is partially boosted by the growth in the number of active cardholders in our sample. This could be due to 
an increasing customer base or inactive customers using their cards more frequently. 
2. Per household card spending growth only looks at households that complete at least five transactions with Bank of America cards in the 
month. Per household spending growth isolates impacts from a changing sample size, which could be unrelated to underlying economic 
momentum, and potential spending volatility from less active users. 
3. Overall total card spending includes small business card spending while per household card spending does not. 
4. Differences due to using processing dates (total card spending) versus transaction date (per household card spending). 
5. Other differences including household formations due to young adults moving in and out of their parent’s houses during COVID. 
Any household consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level. Whenever median household savings and 
checking balances are quoted, the data is based on a fixed cohort of households that had a consumer deposit account (checking and/or savings 
account) for all months from January 2019 through the most current month of data shown.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
We consider a measure of services necessity spending that includes but is not limited to groceries, utilities, and gasoline. Discretionary services 
includes but is not limited to charitable donations, leisure travel, entertainment, and professional/consumer services. Discretionary retail 
includes but is not limited to general merchandise, miscellaneous, clothing, electronics and furniture. It excludes categories like groceries and 
gasoline. Holiday spending is defined as items in which spending in the November-December period is usually at least 20% of total annual 
spending on the category.  Value and premium grocers are determined judgmentally on a proprietary analysis of merchants by equity analysts.  
Durables spending is defined as spending on electronics, building materials, auto-related spending and furniture. Premium durables spending is 
based on a selection of retailers who are judged to sell relatively higher value products. Conversely, value durables spending is based on a 
selection of retailers who are judged to sell relatively lower value products.  
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Any reference to card spending per household on gasoline includes all purchases at gasoline stations and might include purchases of non-gas 
items. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

IMF 罕⻅背书⽇本货币政策正常化，并提⽰若通胀超预期央⾏需加快加息。⽬前⽇本政策利
率已升⾄ 1.25% ，市场预计年内或再加息。随着⽇债收益率上升，⽇本资⾦加速撤离法债
等海外资产，加剧了全球债市的波动与承压。
摘要：
国际货币基⾦组织罕⻅地为⽇本货币政策正常化背书，并警告若通胀超预期，⽇本央⾏可能需
要加快加息步伐。这⼀表态，在全球债市已然动荡之际，令市场对⽇本资⾦撤离海外资产的担
忧进⼀步升温。
IMF 亚太部⻔主任 Krishna Srinivasan 在本周于曼⾕召开的 IMF 年会期间接受路透社采访时表⽰，
⽇本央⾏ " 正在做正确的事 " ，并强调若通胀上升快于预期， " 必须更快收紧，因为你不希望通胀
预期脱锚。这取决于数据。 "⽇本央⾏⾏⻓植⽥和男亦在出席本次 IMF 年会时重申，央⾏将专注
于将核⼼通胀锚定在 2% ⽬标附近，不会容忍任何超调，进⼀步强化了市场对年内再度加息的预
期。
IMF 的表态恰逢全球债市承压之际。法国 10 年期国债收益率⼀度逼近 5% ，创 2002 年以来新⾼，

借贷成本已⾼于希腊和意⼤利；美国 10 年期国债收益率攀升⾄ 5.28% 的数⼗年⾼位，即便通胀
数据⾛软也未能遏制抛售势头。在部分市场分析⼈⼠看来，⽇本政策正常化及由此引发的⽇本
资⾦回流，或是这两场动荡背后不可忽视的结构性⼒量。
⽇本央⾏正常化提速预期升温
⽇本央⾏已于今年 6 ⽉和 9 ⽉相继加息，将政策利率推升⾄ 1.25% ，为 31 年来最⾼。多数分析师
预计年内还将有⾄少⼀次加息。
Srinivasan 指出，⽇元疲软进⼀步推⾼了原材料进⼝成本，加剧了国内通胀压⼒，这也正是促
使⽇本央⾏持续收紧政策的重要背景之⼀。⽇本央⾏本⽉晚些时候将召开关键政策会议，市场
普遍预计届时利率将维持不变，但可能释放鹰派信号，为 12 ⽉加息铺路。
此外，中东冲突带来的通胀⻛险和⼈⼯智能需求旺盛，也在本次 IMF 年会上成为各国政策制定者
讨论的重要议题。
IMF ：⽇元⼲预不违背浮动汇率承诺
在汇率问题上， IMF 对⽇本当局的⽴场给予背书。 Srinivasan 表⽰，⽇本当局 " 完全致⼒于⾃由浮

动汇率制度 " ，他与⽇⽅进⾏了多次讨论，对此 " 毫不怀疑 " 。
IMF 在其亚洲经济报告的分析章节中指出， 2025 年年中以来⽇元的⼤幅波动，很⼤程度上与收
益率差等基本⾯因素关联不⼤，⽽是与⽇元期货的投机性仓位同步移动。⽇本于今年 7 ⽉ 31 ⽇与
美国联合进⾏了⼀次罕⻅的协调⼲预，以阻⽌⽇元跌⾄近 40 年低点。 Srinivasan 表⽰，⽇本汇市
深度充⾜，当局将汇率波动作为 " 第⼀道防线 " ，⼲预仅在特定情况下使⽤。
除货币政策外，⽇本的财政可持续性同样令市场存有疑虑。市场担⼼⽇本政府的⼤规模⽀出计
划，叠加明年 4 ⽉起为期两年的消费税下调，将进⼀步扩⼤本已庞⼤的债务规模。
Srinivasan 承认市场对财政⻛险的担忧，但也指出，疫情以来⽇本的财政整合⼒度实际上已超过
其他任何⼀个 G7 国家，部分原因在于税收收⼊的持续增⻓。他同时强调，⽇本需要明确新⽀出

⽇本资⾦撤离或加剧全球债市波动
全球债市的动荡，与⽇本政策正常化进程之间的关联正受到越来越多的关注。据彭博专栏作家
Gearoid Reidy 在分析⽂章中指出，世界或许并未为 " 有利率的⽇本 " 做好充分准备，⽽法国可能
只是第⼀个受到冲击的市场。
数据显⽰，截⾄今年 7 ⽉，⽇本投资者持有约 23 万亿⽇元（约合 1450 亿美元）法国国债，是⽇本
在欧元区最⼤的超配仓位。然⽽随着⽇本 10 年期国债收益率上⽉升破 3% ，创 30 年新⾼，经汇率
对冲后，法国 10 年期国债相对⽇本国债的收益率优势已收窄⾄约 40 个基点，吸引⼒⼤幅下滑。
收益率优势消退叠加法国财政状况恶化，⽇本资⾦已开始松动。数据显⽰，⽇本持有的法国国

债规模较去年底减少 2.5% 。三井住友 DS 资产管理旗下由 Shinji Kunibe 负责的全球固收基⾦已出
于财政担忧清仓法债。瑞穗的 Masayuki Nakajima 表⽰： " 即便估值看起来更便宜，⽇本投资者
重建仓位的动⼒也在下降。 "
随着 IMF 为⽇本加快加息提供政策⽀撑，⽇本国内资产吸引⼒的持续回升，或将使这⼀趋势延
续，并对依赖⽇本资⾦的海外债券市场形成持续的结构性压⼒。

GROWTH MOMENTUM TO SUSTAIN, 
EXTERNAL RISKS LINGER

India registered  strong growth at the start of 
FY27. Sustaining this performance will depend 
increasingly on how effectively it navigates a 
more uncertain global environment. Besides 
trade fragmentation, geopolitical tensions 
leading to higher energy prices, and shifts in 
international capital flows present key external 
risks.

India joined its Southeast Asian peers in 
posting an upside surprise on growth in 
1QFY27 (Apr -Jun26), up a firm 7.8%  yoy. few 
points of note:  
A) strong 1Q growth print reinforced the view 
that India’s domestic demand cycle was more 
resilient than initially assumed, with 
consumption, public capex and manufacturing 
providing a solid base despite external shocks.  
B) prevailing tailwinds by way of indirect tax 
relief in 4Q25, real rate buffer, and easier 
monetary conditions (earlier cuts), were also 
supportive;  
C) nominal GDP growth returned above 10% 
after two years on higher deflators, though 
some segments like manufacturing faced 
negative deflators as input prices rose at a 
faster pace than output due to commodity price 
increases and import intensity;  
D) Revisions to backdated numbers, to account 
for output PPI series, pushed up FY26 growth to 
7.8% (from 7.7% earlier). Two prior years were 
also revised.

High-frequency indicators

Into July -August, high frequency 
indicators are holding up, including GST 
e-way bills, indirect tax  revenues, 
electricity demand and growth in digital 
payments, while toll collections and 
petroleum consumption slipped . For 
demand, a contained extent of increase 
in retail pump prices shielded 
households from the surge in global 
crude prices, but other market linked 
fuel variants have climbed in recent 
months. On the employment front, job 
reductions were witnessed in the 
software sector, even as fresh 
opportunities were reported in global 
capability centres , electr onics/ 
semiconductors, healthcare, auto/ 
ancillary etc.  Wage growth in services 
has improved , manufacturing has been 
steady, while farm sector moderated, 
according t o consumption surveys by 
CMIE.

Industry indicators are a mixed bag, with 
PMIs maintaining their foothold in the 
expansionary terrain, while production 
and eight core index growth come off 
highs. Auto production continues to 
expand, though output and sales might 
moderate in the next quarter on base 
effects due to indirect tax cuts in the 
same time year ago. Forward-looking 
firm surveys have improved, after 
initially coming under pressure.

Sectors associated with rural demand  
might moderate amid weaker kharif 
sowing activity and lower reservoir 
levels impacting the rabi crop activity. 
Cumulative southwest monsoon is ~15% 
below the long-term average by tail end 
of the season. This takes the country on 
course to face its driest monsoon since 
2009 (which was 18% below LPA). 
Uneven geographical spread is notable, 
with south and east India registering 
nearly 25% shortfall.  While k harif 
sowing is on track with 99% of acreage 
completed, the winter crop might come 
under pressure as weather 
developments suggest El Niño  could 
extend into 2027.

### Page 3

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            3

With nominal GDP growth to track 11-12% 
this fiscal yea r, corporate earning s 
growth is expected to fare better than 
recent few years. 1QFY27 performance 
had already set off the year on a strong 
note, barring selected sectors like O&G 
which faced sequential marginal 
pressures from operating costs. Add to 
this, the centre’s capex gr owth is in firm 
double-digits, also backed by growth in 
core imports (non-oil non-gold).

On the external sector , despite better 
performance by exports, oil and 
electronics purchases have weighed on 
the goods math. The trade deficit is set 
to stay wide amidst elevated global 
crude cost, higher industrial fuels, 
cooking gas etc,  even as a wider mix of 
suppliers have addressed energy 
shortages. Notably, the net electronics 
deficit has widened considerably, just 
as exports have improved. Likewise, core 
imports (ex oil ex gold) have stayed firm, 
which partly feeds into higher exports as 
well as broader domestic demand. 
Monthly service trade surplus has been 
rangebound around $17 -18bn in recent 
months dwarfed by $25 -$30bn goods 
gap.

Maintaining growth forecasts

High frequency indicators put 2QFY 
growth close to 7%. We expect full year 
FY27 growth to average 7.3% yoy  from 
a revised 7.8% in FY26 , assuming 
moderation in the momentum in the 
second half of the year on tighter policy 
conditions, lagged impact of high energy 
prices and base effects from indirect tax 
cuts last year fade.  Assuming some 
spillover impact of exogenous 
uncertainties and tight financial 
conditions at home, we expect FY28 
growth to average 6.8-7.0%.

INFLATION OFF ITS BACK – 
THREE CATALYSTS

After two years of benign inflation, 
India’s price trend is off its back this 
year, mainly on the back of three

### Page 4

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            4

catalysts: Food, energy and early signs 
of demand pass through.

Aug inflation rose to a 20 -month high of 
4.8% yoy from 4.5% month before . As 
the chart highlights, number of sub -
segments growing by faster than the 
inflation target at 4% have continued to 
climb, pointing to a broadening out in the 
momentum.

Food inflation is gradually becoming 
more broad -based, shifting beyond 
vegetables and pulses to sugar, milk, 
protein groups, edible oils, amongst 
others. Sub-par weather has contributed 
to this upmove, besides idiosyncratic 
factors like swing in production  trends, 
diversion for fuel usage etc. After two 
years of normal monsoon, cumulative 
southwest rainfall this year is currently 
15% below the long -term average, 
alongside slower build -up in reservoir 
levels, which could impact upcoming rabi 
crops as well.  El-Nino patterns continue 
to strengthen, adding to seasonal swings 
and risk of impacting farm output.

Beyond food, g lobal oil prices have 
averaged ~$91pb in CY26, staying above 
$100pb for much of Seo26 . Retail fuel 
prices were adjusted in quick succession 
back in May26 and have been left 
unchanged since then , while CNG, 
aviation fuel, LPG etc have  continued to 
climb.

Transport inflation jumped to 4.6% yoy in 
Aug vs 0.1% in Jan26, besides utilities’ 
component inching up. Business price 
expectation surveys (conducted by IIM 
Ahmedabad) have, nonetheless, 
captured businesses facing a notable 
increase in fuel costs, especially 
industrial fuels, base metals and 
derivative product groups. Imported 
price pressures are most notabl e in the 
producer price/ wholesale price indices. 
These gauges have been hovering at 9 -
10% yoy in recent months.

### Page 5

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            5

Lastly, core inflation  (ex-food and fuel)  
rose to 1.5-month high in August, ma inly 
due to a sequential rebound in precious 
metals – gold and silver jewellery. 
Excluding precious metals, the reading 
was more moderate at 2.9%, pointing to 
some extent of economic slack. 
Nonetheless, other pockets like telecom, 
restaurants, business services, transport 
fares etc have been inching up, 
reflecting early signs of broadening in 
price pressures (on sequential basis), 
warranting attention. These are set 
against the backdrop of strong bank 
credit growth, flush INR liquidity and 
resilience in headline growth.

Our correlation analysis between 
liquidity (as % of NDTL) and core inflation 
– see chart, points to a positive but 
modest corelation at 0.4 between the 
two variables, with an assumed year -
worth lag on inflation. The inflation -
fighting mandate will also pus h the 
central bank to reduce the surplus 
liquidity in the domestic system.

A sh arp in global commodity prices is 
expected to fe ed into the wholesale 
price gauge , pushing up reading s to 
double-digits next quarter.

A gradual broadening of price 
pressures is likely to keep headline 
inflation above 5% in second half of the 
fiscal year , sharply narrowing the real 
rate buffer, underscoring the need for a 
tighter policy bias. Add to this, recent 
developments, including a sustained rise 
in crude prices, tightening global 
financial conditions, firm domestic 
growth and signs of broadening in core 
pressures, strengthen the case for a  
shallow 50bp hike in second half of FY27, 
making October’s meeting a live one.

TACKLING LIQUIDITY DELUGE, 
HIGHER YIELDS

Beating street estimates by a wide 
margin, the RBI’s special swap windows 
raised a cumulative $ 143bn into Sep , 
with the highest contribution (~92%) by

### Page 6

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            6

FCNR(B) deposits at $1 33bn, while rest 
were via offshore borrowing facilities. 
These flows pushed up the foreign 
reserves stock to a fresh high past 
$780bn, providing considerable 
firepower to defend the currency. This is 
already noticeable in the recent shift in 
the intervention bias. USDINR, which was 
earlier stubbornly steady in the face of 
strong FCNR inflows but  eased sharply 
due to strong intervention dollar sales 
and broader dollar swings, before being 
led higher due to external triggers.

Liquidity is a key watch factor. Given the 
swap arrangement, these inflows have 
added to already abundant INR liquidity 
backdrop, which was at a four year high, 
depressing overnight rates. The banking 
system surplus jumped past INR 10trn 
surpassing 2022 and Covid highs, with 
the durable balance widening sharply 
higher to INR 14trn, suppressing 
overnight call rates. This compares to 
average INR 1trn surplus in late-June.

While organic drivers like tax -related 
outflows, and seasonal currency leakage, 
in addition to CAD, portfolio outflows, 
and maturity of the forwards book will 
act as counter -balancing factors, yet 
concerted steps were required to drain 
the potential surge in liquidity.

We discussed the various seasonal, 
near-term and permanent measures  
that the central bank might consider (see 
FCNR bonanza necessitates deft 
management) to absorb this surfeit. 
Policymakers are being mindful of both 
the liquidity and signalling effects of 
the sterilisation tools at their disposal. 
The RBI utilised tranches of market 
operations like VRRR across tenors, 
after which three tranches of open 
market operations (bonds maturing FY29 
and FY32) worth a cumulative INR 1trn 
were conducted. These announcements 
fuelled a rise in yields, which were 
already inching up, tracking global 
counterparts. Sell/buy FX swaps for  
shorter rollovers were also being 
reportedly tapped.

When the dust settles, focus will also 
be on the bunched -up maturities that 
will fall due in 3Y and 5Y tenor of the 
deposits. A portion of the existing 
reserve stock could be earmarked 
against these liabilities, helping to 
mitigate concerns that deposit 
maturities or debt repayments could 
trigger a sharp increase in dollar demand 
and exert pressure on the FX market 
down the line. In the near-term, priorities 
will be to manage liquidity, gradually 
lower the sizeable forwards book, and 
support the domestic currency. Long -
end yields continue to be influenced by 
the hardening global yields.

BALANCE OF PAYMENTS MATH 
TO BE COMFORTABLE

The FY27 current account math is 
expected to display two key counter 
trends, Firstly, the wider goods trade 
imbalance will raise the FY27 current 
account deficit. Secondly, the 
counterbalancing effect of a sharp 
improvement in the capital flows math 
will keep the annual balance of 
payments math in surplus.

### Page 7

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            7

India recorded a current account deficit 
(CAD) of $10.5bn during April-July 2026, 
compared with $6.1bn in the 
corresponding period last year. The 
deterioration was driven almost entirely 
by a wider merchandise trade deficit, 
which rose to $117.8bn from $97.1bn a 
year ago. Part of this offset by higher  
income, transfers and capital account 
flow. Capital flows found support from 
net FDI, portfolio investments and 
banking capital, especially NRI deposits 
(jumped sharply to $36bn vs $4.6bn 
same period last year) , pushing up Apr -
Jul26 BOP to $12.7bn surplus, more than 
double of $4.8bn same time last year.

Oil prices are the main swing factor for 
the CAD this year , with the current run -
rate tracking our forecast at -1.1% of GDP 
for FY27. This suggests that the 
widening in the CAD is commodity -price 
related rather than driven by higher 
demand.

On the other hand, capital flows are 
being buttressed by the special swap 
windows introduced by the central bank, 
which raised a cumulative $ 143bn 
between June and August, through non -
resident deposits and external 
borrowings. This has helped to fil l the 
gap from subdued portfolio flows and 
net investment trends. We expect FY27 
current account deficit to track -1.1% of 
GDP in FY27, with the balance of 
payments to register upwards of $80bn 
surplus (vs -$23.6bn deficit last year).  
Into FY28, the dependency of capital 
flows on o rganic drivers like portfolio 
flows and FDI is set to increase, 
underscoring the need for higher 
vigilance.

FISCAL RUN-RATE

The FY27 fiscal run -rate points to a 
stronger revenue momentum, which 
has helped to keep the FYTD deficit in 
check, even as expenditure was 
frontloaded owing to subsidy payments 
and capex disbursements. April-July 
2026 deficit stands at a third of the full-
year balance.

### Page 8

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            8

Both tax and non -tax receipts fared 
better than the comparable period year 
ago, helped by stronger corporate tax 
collections, STT transaction revenues, 
and RBI dividend, amongst others.

Setting 2QFY for a strong start, net 
direct tax collections r ose 13% yoy by 
mid-Sep26, driven by net corporate tax 
collections up 19.5%. Net non -corporate 
tax collections were up 6%, besides STT 
collections up a sharp 53% yoy. Add to 
this, divestments have also fared better 
than expected this year, with ~78% 
raised by August, accompanied by 
dividends & profits (at 83% of budget by 
Jul26).

Under expenditure, about 33% of the full 
year allocation was allocated by July 
2026. The subsidy outgo rose sharply, up 
35% yoy, led by higher food at 23% and 
fertiliser at 46% (already accounting for 
nearly two-third of the full year budget)  
led by surging global DAP and urea 
prices. With global prices remaining high, 
the actual outgo might be higher than 
budgeted INR 1.7trn , likely towards INR 
2.5trn. Capital spending also remained 
apace, up 30% yoy in first five months of 
the year.

Overall, strong growth in tax and non-tax 
receipts allowed the centre to sustain 
higher spending while containing the 
extent of fiscal slippage until July 2026 
(FYTD). A strong nominal GDP rate 
(helped by higher inflation) will also 
provide a favourable denominator effect.

IN SUM

The economy exhibited resilience in the 
face of a tough external environment in 
the first quarter, with part of that 
stability likely to extend to 2QFY27 as 
well. Tighter monetary conditions, El 
Nino impact and base effects from 
passage of post -indirect tax jump will 
moderate headline growth in 2HFY. 
Nonetheless for the year, we expected 
growth to stay north of 7%, factoring in a 
strong start.

THIS WEEK IN EARNINGS 
AGGREGATE ESTIMATES AND REVISIONS 
 26Q3 Y/Y earnings are expected to be 30.6%. Excluding the energy sector, the Y/Y earnings estimate is 26.8%. 
 Of the 13 companies in the S&P 500 that have reported earnings to date for 26Q3, 84.6% have reported earnings 
above analyst estimates. This compares to a long-term average of 67.7% and prior four quarter average of 81.5%. 
 26Q3 Y/Y revenue is expected to be 11.6%. Excluding the energy sector, the growth estimate is 11.2%. 
 76.9% of companies have reported 26Q3 revenue above analyst expectations. This compares to a long-term 
average of 63% and an average over the past four quarters of 76.8%. 
 For 26Q3, there have been 40 negative EPS preannouncements issued by S&P 500 corporations compared to 65 
positive EPS preannouncements. By dividing 40 by 65 the N/P ratio is 0.6 for the S&P 500 Index.  
 The forward four-quarter (26Q4– 27Q3) P/E ratio for the S&P 500 is 20.1. 
 During the week of Oct. 5, three S&P 500 companies are expected to report quarterly earnings.

There has been an increase in the share-weighted earnings for the S&P 500 since the start of the quarter (to $773.2B 
from $760.7B). Seven of the eleven sectors have experienced downward revisions to estimates.

Since Aug. 1, the materials (-9.1%) and health care (-2.2%) sectors have recorded the highest percentage decreases 
in earnings, while the energy (16.3%) sector has recorded the highest percentage increase in earnings. Overall, share-
weighted earnings for the S&P 500 have increased by 1.6% since the start of the quarter.

Since Aug. 1, the health care (-1.7B) and materials (-1.5B) sectors have recorded the highest dollar-level decreases in 
earnings, while the information technology (8.3B) sector has recorded the highest dollar-level increase in earnings. 
Overall, expected share-weighted earnings for the S&P 500 have increased by 12.5B since the start of the quarter.

13 companies in the S&P 500 Index have reported earnings for Q3 2026. Of these companies, 84.6% reported 
earnings above analyst expectations and 7.7% reported earnings below analyst expectations. In a typical quarter 
(since 1994), 67% of companies beat estimates and 20% miss estimates. Over the past four quarters, 82% of 
companies beat the estimates and 15% missed estimates. 
In aggregate, companies are reporting earnings that are 5.3% above estimates, which compares to a long-term (since 
1994) average surprise factor of 4.4% and the average surprise factor over the prior four quarters of 7.7%.

The estimated earnings growth rate for the S&P 500 for 26Q3 is 30.6%. If the energy sector is excluded, the growth 
rate declines to 26.8%. The S&P 500 expects to see share-weighted earnings of $773.2B in 26Q3, compared to share-
weighted earnings of $591.9B (based on the year-ago earnings of the current 504 constituents) in 25Q3. 
11 of the 11 sectors in the index expect to see an improvement in earnings relative to 25Q3. The Energy and 
Information Technology sectors have the highest earnings growth rates for the quarter, while the Consumer Staples 
sector has the weakest anticipated growth compared to 25Q3. 
The Energy sector has the highest earnings growth rate (114.7%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $55.1B in 26Q3, 
compared to earnings of $25.7B in 25Q3. 4 of the 5 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see higher earnings 
than a year ago. The Oil & Gas Refining & Marketing (445.6%) and Integrated Oil & Gas (116.1%) sub-industries have 
the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate declines to 25.1%. 
The Information Technology sector has the second highest earnings growth rate (66.5%) of any sector. It is expected 
to earn $256.1B in 26Q3, compared to earnings of $153.8B in 25Q3. 12 of the 12 sub-industries in the sector are 
anticipated to see higher earnings than a year ago. The Semiconductors (135.6%) and Semiconductor Materials & 
Equipment (68.9%) sub-industries have the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, 
the growth rate declines to 25.3%. 
The Consumer Staples sector has the lowest earnings growth rate (3.2%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $33.7B 
in 26Q3, compared to earnings of $32.6B in 25Q3. 3 of the 10 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see lower 
earnings than a year ago. The Packaged Foods & Meats (-6.5%) and Household Products (-4.2%) sub-industries have 
the lowest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate improves to 6.4%.

The Information Technology sector has the second highest earnings growth rate (54.9%) of any sector. It is expected 
to earn $289.1B in 26Q4, compared to earnings of $186.6B in 25Q4. 12 of the 12 sub-industries in the sector are 
anticipated to see higher earnings than a year ago. The Semiconductors (117%) and Semiconductor Materials & 
Equipment (68.8%) sub-industries have the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, 
the growth rate declines to 18.4%. 
The Real Estate sector has the lowest earnings growth rate (4.2%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $15.2B in 
26Q4, compared to earnings of $14.6B in 25Q4. 2 of the 13 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see lower 
earnings than a year ago. The Telecom Tower Reits (-13.3%) and Multi-Family Residential Reits (-0.1%) sub-industries 
have the lowest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate improves to 7.1%.

The estimated revenue growth rate for the S&P 500 for 26Q3 is 11.6%. If the energy sector is excluded, the growth rate declines  to 11.2%. The S&P 
500 expects to see revenue of $4826.7B in 26Q3, compared to revenue of $4324.7B (based on the year-ago earnings of the current 504 
constituents) in 25Q3.

11 of the 11 sectors anticipate revenue growth for the quarter. The Information Technology sector has the highest revenue growth rate for the 
quarter, while the Health Care sector has the weakest anticipated growth compared to 25Q3.

The Information Technology sector has the highest revenue growth rate (39.5%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $764.6B in 26Q3, compared to 
revenue of $547.9B in 25Q3. 12 of the 12 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see higher revenue than a year ago. The Semiconductors 
(87.7%) and Semiconductor Materials & Equipment (48.2%) sub-industries have the highest revenue growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are 
removed, the growth rate declines to 22.1%.

The Health Care sector has the lowest revenue growth rate (4%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $958.4B in 26Q3, compared to revenue of 
$921.2B in 25Q3. 1 of the 10 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see lower revenue than a year ago. The Health Care Supplies (-3.1%) 
and Managed Health Care (1.2%) sub-industries have the lowest revenue growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed , the growth rate 
improves to 5.1%.

STARMINE EARNINGS SURPRISE FORECAST
Looking forward at quarterly performance, we use StarMine’s SmartEstimate® from LSEG to determine which
companies in the S&P 500 are better poised to beat and miss earnings estimates. The SmartEstimate® is a weighted

average of analyst estimates, with more weight given to more recent estimates and more accurate analysts. Our
studies have shown that when the SmartEstimate® differs significantly from the consensus (I/B/E/S mean estimate),
the Predicted Surprise accurately predicts the direction of earnings surprises or further revisions 70% of the time.
When a significant Predicted Surprise for revenue is also present for the period, the accuracy improves to 78%.

StarMine ARM from LSEG is an analyst revisions stock ranking model, designed to predict future changes in analyst
sentiment. ARM incorporates more accurate earnings estimates through the SmartEstimate prediction service. ARM
region rankings scores companies by region on a scale of 1 to 100 where 100 represents the most bullish sentiment.
Over the next two weeks, 31 S&P 500 companies are expected to report earnings. Of these companies, three positive

surprises and five negative surprises are expected from S&P 500 companies reporting quarterly results.

Document 288: Next Generation EU_ The programme is coming to an end, but its effects on economic activity will endure (2026-09-23)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Next Generation EU_ The programme is coming to an end, but its effects on economic activity will endure (2026-09-23).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-09-23
Pages: 3
Status: ok

### Page 1

23 September 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
NEXT GENERATION EU: THE PROGRAMME IS COMING TO AN END, BUT ITS EFFECTS ON ECONOMIC ACTIVITY WILL
ENDURE
Guillaume Derrien
A pillar of the NextGenerationEU programme, the Recovery and Resilience Facility (RRF) will expire on December 31, 2026. As of the
end of August 2026, slightly more than three-quarters of the total budget had been disbursed (EUR 440 billion out of EUR 573 billion).
Disbursing the remaining quarter (EUR 133 billion) by the end of the year will require a significant increase compared with the histo-

rical pace (EUR 90 billion per year). The goal of channeling these funds toward productive investment appears to have been achieved.
Tangible results for investment in Europe
According to Eurostat, public spending co-financed by the RRF totaled EUR 310 billion between 2020 and 2025. Nearly three-quarters (74%)
consisted of capital expenditures, including EUR 106 billion in direct public investment and EUR 122 billion in capital transfers to the private

sector (such as grants). This spending supported investment by businesses and households.
The list of major programmes funded by the RFF, published by the European Commission 1, shows that, within this scope, nearly two-thirds of
the funding went to transportation (primarily rail), energy infrastructure, and digital infrastructure (see Chart 1).
Another notable point: capital expenditures co-financed by the RRF increased significantly, reaching 0.4% of GDP for the European Union as a

whole in 2025 (see Chart 2). There are significant disparities among Member States, primarily due to differences in the amounts allocated to
them. Croatia (1.9%), Greece (1.2%), Italy (1%), and Spain (0.7%) received significant support, in contrast to France and Germany (0.1%). These
figures are consistent with the IMF’s estimate that the RRF added approximately 0.3 percentage points (pp) to EU growth in 2025 (more than
1 pp in Greece and Croatia, around 0.8 pp in Italy, and 0.5 pp in Spain) 2. Furthermore, as the first major joint European borrowing initiative, the

RRF made it possible to finance investment without increasing national budget deficits, giving several countries (Spain, Italy, Greece, Portugal)
valuable breathing room to consolidate their public finances.
1 Recovery and Resilience Scoreboard
2 The EU’s Recovery and Resilience Facility: Experience and Lessons for the Next Multiannual Financial Framework, WP/26/114, June 2026
SOURCE: EUROPEAN COMMISSION, Recovery and Resilience Scorecard

Chart of the Week / 23 September 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
What impact on EU’s economic activity can we expect once this programme ends?
At first glance, effects will be limited. The end of the programme is expected to weigh on both public and private investment. However, the
strengthening of economic activity that we anticipate (see details in our latest Ecoperspectives – Q3 2026) rests primarily on German public

investment programmes and structural drivers (investment in AI, defence spending).
The ECB makes the same point in its September macroeconomic projections 3. It emphasises that the end of the RRF is expected to weigh
on public investment in 2027, while it anticipates an acceleration in business investment. Furthermore, RRF funds are being absorbed on
the ground only gradually. Since most disbursements are concentrated toward the end of the programme, the effects of the RRF will extend

beyond 2026.

Document 315: The Commodities Feed_ Oil holds firm as Middle East breakthrough remains elusive

Source PDF: all report pdf/The Commodities Feed_ Oil holds firm as Middle East breakthrough remains elusive.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-16
Pages: 4
Status: ok

### Page 1

Oil is holding ﬁrm as Middle East tensions refuse to cool.
Meanwhile, IEA members agreed to release the rest of the
emergency stockpiles they pledged earlier this year

Warren Patterson and Ewa Manthey
Energy – IEA member to release remaining
oil
Oil remains well-supported. A pattern has set in: prices slide early in
the session, then claw back most of those losses by the close. As a
result, ICE Brent continues to comfortably trade above $100/bbl.
Uncertainty in the Middle East and concerns over the sustainability
of oil ﬂows through the Strait of Hormuz has the market pricing in a
signiﬁcant risk premium. The longer this pickup in ﬂows continues,
the more likely the market will start giving back some of this risk
premium. In order to see a full removal, there needs to be some kind
of agreement between the US and Iran. This would provide the
market with more conﬁdence that stronger oil ﬂows from the region
will continue.

### Page 2

International Energy Agency members agreed to release the
remaining 100m barrels of oil from strategic reserves. It's part of the
initial 400m barrels that members agreed back in March to help
with the Persian Gulf supply shortfall. According to the IEA,
members have distributed around 330m barrels to date. From the
100m barrels to be released, 20m barrels will be diesel, and will
make its way onto the market as soon as possible. The US probably
won’t be happy with the scale of the release, particularly regarding

diesel. Initial suggestions were that we could see around 50m
barrels of diesel released. The risk is that if the US believes these
volumes are not enough, the administration may mull restrictions
on US diesel exports once again.
The Financial Times reports that the US is considering easing
sanctions on Russia’s shadow tanker ﬂeet in bid to reduce fuel
costs. Tanker freight rates surged to record levels recently due to the
growing ineﬃciency in trade ﬂows out of the Persian Gulf. Since the

start of the Russia-Ukraine war, Russia has built a signiﬁcant ﬂeet of
tankers, which are largely sanctioned. The idea is that easing
sanctions on these vessels would free up tanker capacity for the
broader market. The key question is how eﬀective it might be in
bringing down spot tanker rates. It’s unclear how much of this
capacity is actually available. Given UK and EU sanctions, insurance
could still be an issue.
The latest data from the EIA shows that US commercial crude oil

inventories fell by 8.56m barrels over the last week, which is the
largest weekly decline since February. Hurricane activity in the Gulf
played part in the inventory decline, with oﬀshore production shut
in due to the storm. US output is estimated to have fallen by 300k
b/d week-on-week. Crude oil imports fell by 791k b/d WoW, a key
driver behind the fall in stocks. For reﬁned products, US reﬁneries
increased their utilisation rates by 0.8pp to 93.5%, leaving

utilisation rates at their highest seasonal levels in at least 20 years.
Despite this, distillate fuel stocks still fell by 2.53m barrels over the
last week. This leaves distillate inventories at record seasonal lows
as we head into winter. Meanwhile, gasoline stocks increased by
3.52m barrels.
